values reported in
[
33
,
34
]
. The dot-dashed line in panel (b) is the pair threshold in the 3D
tetragonal

UV
model with
V_{z}=0
and
t_{z}=0.01\,t
, see Eq. (
235
).

Rigorous determination of clustering thresholds and phase boundaries requires solving a few-body or a full many-body problem on an infinite lattice with sufficient accuracy, and is therefore difficult. Berciu reported
[
145
]
a zero region of pair stability in a system of 3 to 5
spinless
fermions with nearest-neighbor attraction in an infinite 1D lattice. However, with second-nearest-neighbor
repulsion
turned on, the pairs stabilized in a wide interval of parameters. In this model, pairs need to be stabilized dynamically because Emin’s argument does not apply to spinless fermions. Also in 1D, Chakraborty, Tezuka and Min investigated four “extended-Holstein bipolarons” on chains up to 24 sites long
[
146
]
. At high phonon frequencies, this model
[
137
]
maps to a
UV
model. The authors reported a narrow but finite interval of bipolaron stability in qualitative agreement with the 1D
UV
phase diagram shown on Fig.
26
(a) and to be discussed below.

In 2D, Emery, Kivelson, and Lin reported
[
33
]
pair stability in the interval
2.0\,t<|V|<3.53\,t
for a
4\times 4
lattice and
U=\infty
. A similar interval,
2.0\,t<|V|<3.8\,t
on a
4\times 4
lattice, was reported by Dagotto and Riera for the
t-J
model
[
34
]
.

Multi-magnon bound states in a 2D square frustrated magnetic were analyzed in
[
86
]
.

We are unaware of any numerical investigation of clustering in 3D lattices except our own work
[
147
]
to be discussed next.

We now summarize our own results on pair liquid stability in
UV
models
[
35
,
148
,
149
,
147
]
. First off,
bosonic

UV
models are unstable: as soon as pairs form, trions
[
148
]
and quads
[
35
]
form as well, triggering phase separation. The same conclusion applies to
spinless fermions
: those pairs are unstable, too. Phase diagrams of four
spinful fermions
in 1D and 2D are shown in Fig.
26
(a) and Fig.
26
(b), respectively
[
35
]
. In 1D, the pair stability region is narrow; it shrinks to zero in the
U\rightarrow\infty
limit. In 2D, the pair stability region is
\approx 2t
wide for all
U
. In 3D, exact solutions are computationally more demanding. Only a three-fermion problem has been solved in a 3D
UV
model so far
[
147
]
. Figure
27
shows the phase diagram of three fermions in the tetragonal
UV
model for
U=10\,t
. Pairs are stable against formation of trions between the solid and dashed lines. One important feature of Fig.
27
is the sharp increase of
V_{2}
for small but nonzero
t_{z}
. Going back to Fig.
26
(b), one should be careful when interpreting the pure 2D case as an approximation to a highly anisotropic 3D case. Even a small
t_{z}
elevates the
V_{2}
line and significantly reduces the domain of pair stability, which is illustrated by the
t_{z}=0.01\,t
line. Although the domain of pair stability shrinks significantly upon turning on interlayer hopping,
the former remains finite for all
U
.

Figure 27:
Phase diagram of three fermions in the 3D tetragonal
UV
model for
U=10\,t

[
147
]
. The pairs are stable against formation of trions between the
V_{2}
and
V_{3}
lines. The trion threshold
V_{3}
was computed on a
12\times 12\times 12
lattice. The dot-dashed line marks the coordinates of
peak
close-packed BEC temperature,
{\cal T}^{\ast}_{\rm BEC}
, determined by locating the maxima of plots like those shown in Fig.
24
.

A high-level conclusion from this analysis is the existence of finite domains of pair stability in various forms of the
UV
model in all lattice dimensionalities.
It is within such domains is where the results derived in the bulk of this paper are valid.
The fundamental reason for pair stability is the Fermi statistics and associated nodes in the many-fermion wave functions
[
144
,
35
]
. Since these reasons are general and applicable to any system, we expect them to hold for other types of potentials including, for example, large-radius attractions considered in Section
5.4
.

12
Summary and discussion

12.1
Common properties of lattice bound pairs

In this paper,
UV
models (extended attractive Hubbard models) of increasing complexity have been analyzed in detail. Taken together, the results point to several common properties of lattice bound states. They are summarized in this section.

12.1.1
Mathematically exact solutions

The most important property of two-particle lattice problems is their exact solvability. This is a big advantage as all the results can be easily verified and cross-checked, both analytically and numerically. When it comes to physical applications, typical arguments are centered around justifications of a starting Hamiltonian or relevancy to a particular real system. But once the model is agreed upon, the conclusions that follow are not usually questioned.

At the same time, the complexity of a solution increases rapidly with interaction range and lattice dimensionality. As shown in Section
2.2
, the size of a matrix that defines pair dispersion, Eq. (
17
), is equal to the number of interacting lattice sites. Separation into singlet and triplet states described in Sections
2.3
and
2.4
cuts the matrix size by about half, which is helpful. Still, the ability to derive analytical results is largely limited to nearest-neighbor interactions. The longer-range square model considered in Section
5.4
and Ref.
[
99
]
is a rare exception. Additionally, the matrix elements
M
that populate Eq. (
17
) are two-body lattice Green’s functions whose complexity increases with lattice dimensionality. In general,
M
’s can be analytically evaluated in 1D, see Eqs. (
65
) and (
360
)-(
364
), and in 2D, see
B
. In 3D, analytical expressions are only known for the isotropic simple cubic lattice,
D.1
. In other cases, numerical integration is necessary. The ability to perform two integrations analytically, as shown in
D.3
, reduces
M
’s to one-dimensional integrals which makes subsequent numerics efficient.

The exact dispersion equation defines pair energy over the entire BZ of pair momenta. It provides singlet–triplet splitting, energy gaps between various sub-bands of the same parity, and a total bandwidth. The dispersion possesses degeneracies at high-symmetry points, which can be cross-checked with group theory classifications. By setting the pair energy equal to the lowest energy of two free particles
with the same total momentum
a binding threshold is determined. At this special energy, matrix elements
M
simplify considerably. In many cases, and especially at the
\Gamma
point and along BZ diagonals, the thresholds can be calculated analytically. Many explicit examples have been derived in this paper.

The exact solution also provides pair effective mass and radius. The mass is rigorously defined via a second derivative of the total energy near the band minimum and as such it can be extracted from the dispersion relation. In some cases, one can utilize the fact that the dispersion relation simplifies along BZ diagonals and derive analytical expressions. See, for example, Es. (
77
). Calculation of effective radius is more involved as it requires knowledge or the entire pair wave function and its subsequent integration. Still, analytic expressions have been obtained in simple cases, see Eqs. (
71
) and (
80
). In more complicated models, analytical manipulations enable reductions of multidimensional integrals to one-dimensional integrals, as was done, for example, in Ref.
[
32
]
. The effective radius yields the pair volume and the
close-packed density of pairs
which is an important parameter in the theory of Bose-Einstein condensation of pairs. Together with pair mass and full dispersion, it provides estimates of the close-packed BEC temperatures and enables conclusions about optimal model parameters.

12.1.2
Pair binding energy depends on its momentum

This is purely a lattice effect that is absent in continuous space
[
1
]
. Since the lattice provides a reference frame, movement through it is not Galilean-invariant. Binding energies and masses of bound complexes become dependent on their momenta
{\bf P}
. The pair energy and the minimum energy of two free particles both rise with
{\bf P}
but the latter rises faster. It means that the binding energy
increases
with
{\bf P}
. In systems with a finite threshold, it leads to situations when there are no bound pairs at
{\bf P}=0
but one or more bound pair at finite
{\bf P}
. A similar effect has long been known in the theory of quantum spin waves
[
79
]
. Increasing stability of pairs can also be seen in threshold formulas with explicit dependence on
{\bf P}
, see for example Eqs. (
125
), (
159
), and (
226
), where thresholds decrease to zero in BZ corners. That implies there is
always
at least one bound pair in BZ corners for
any

U
as long as
|V|>0
.

An important consequence is the existence of a
binding surface
in momentum space which separates unbound and bound pairs. As shown in Section
5.2
, the binding surface does not in general match the Fermi surface of
free
electrons. The mismatch leads to segmentation of the Fermi surface into areas that are more or less prone to pairing and potential appearance of Fermi arcs. We note that such a pair would have formed with a nonzero total momentum. This is opposite to conventional Cooper pairs that are formed with zero total momentum. The described effect may also have links to the FFLO phase. A key question is how the binding surface is modified by a finite fermion density. This is a many-body problem that goes beyond the scope of this work.

12.1.3
Light pairs

Effective mass is an important characteristic of a bound pair. Broadly, it defines pair mobility and response to external perturbations. In the context of superfluidity, the mass determines a BEC temperature in a system of many pairs, as argued in the Introduction. It is important to know what makes pairs either light or heavy.

Near threshold, the mass is always close to two free-particle masses. In the opposite, strong coupling limit, the mass generally scales as
m^{\ast}_{p}/m_{0}\propto|V|/t
, i.e., increases linearly with the binding energy. This is because the pair moves in the second order in hopping
t
and breaks an attractive bond in the intermediate state, see Fig.
8
(a). However, there are two circumstances when a pair can move in the
first
order in
t
and the above scaling is not followed. Instead, pair mass remains of order
m_{0}
at all energies including the strong coupling limit. We refer to such pairs as
light
.

The first situation arises for purely geometric reasons. One pair member can hop to a neighbor site without breaking an attractive bond. In other words, both the starting site and the ending site are nearest neighbors to the second pair member. Then the second member moves in the same way and the process repeats. The constituent particles hop in turns and the entire pair moves through the lattice in a “crab-like” fashion without ever breaking a bond. A good example is provided by the triangular lattice investigated in Section
7.1
(see also Refs.
[
136
,
138
]
). Pair movement is illustrated in Fig.
16
and the mass is plotted in Fig.
18
. Observe that even at extreme attractive strengths,
|V|>60\,t
, the pair mass remains below
5m_{0}
. For more realistic attractions,
|V|<10\,t
, the mass is below
3m_{0}
. That is, mass increase due to binding is less than 50%. It was suggested in Ref.
[
138
]
in the context of bipolaron superconductivity that for that very reason triangular and hexagonal lattices may host high-temperature superconductivity. Other lattices that support crab motion and light pairs include staggered ladders
[
57
,
135
,
134
]
(
m^{\ast}_{p}<4m_{0}
), staggered square planes (Section
10.1
,
m^{\ast}_{p}<4m_{0}
), and face-centered cubic lattice
[
90
]
(
m^{\ast}_{p}<6m_{0}
). One should add to this list the
body-centered
tetragonal lattice that supports light pairs in
xy
plane but not in
z
direction. This lattice is of interest because of potential connection to cuprate superconductivity. Light pairs in this lattice are yet to be studied. Inclusion of second-neighbor hopping induces light pairs in other lattices
[
58
,
133
,
134
]
.

The second situation leading to light pairs arises when two or more lattice sites have equal attractive potentials. One such model was considered in Section
4.2
. If the on-site interaction is attractive rather than repulsive and
U=V
, then a first-order resonant motion is possible as illustrated in Fig.
8
(b). The analysis is readily generalized to other lattices that do not support light pairs for geometric reasons, see Sections
10.2
and
10.3
. The pair effective mass is
m^{\ast}_{p}<2\sqrt{z}\,m_{0}
, where
z
is the number of nearest neighbors in a lattice. Such a potential is unlikely to arise in a solid crystal but can be engineered in cold gases where
U
and
V
are controlled independently
[
69
,
70
,
71
,
72
]
. Another class of potentials involves equal-strength attractions at finite separation between particles. We considered a 1D example in Section
4.2
, see Fig.
8
(c), and a 2D example in Section
5.4
. In both cases,
m^{\ast}_{p}<4\,m_{0}
was found. One might think that such conditions require fine-tuning and is therefore unlikely in real systems. However, as was argued in Section
5.4
, realistic potentials are combinations of decaying repulsions and long-range attractions. They necessarily have broad and shallow attractive minima. In those situations, the likelihood of two sites near the minimum to have equal attractive strengths is quite high. More instances of this mechanism are investigated in Ref.
[
128
]
.

An overall conclusion of this analysis is that in many circumstances, binding does not lead to significant increase of pair mass. Pair mass is not an impediment for a high mobility or a high BEC temperature.

12.1.4
Anisotropic 3D pairs

The role played by lattice anisotropy is important in the context of cuprate superconductivity. All cuprate superconductors have a layered structure which has led to a popular point of view that the superconductivity is essentially two-dimensional. A number of pure 2D theoretical models have been proposed and extensively studied. In the preformed pair mechanism, however, superconductivity is still considered three-dimensional albeit highly anisotropic. Investigations conducted in the present work support this picture. We analyzed two 3D anisotropic models: tetragonal attractive Hubbard model in Section
3.6
and tetragonal
UV
model in Section
9
. We found in that in both cases the pair formation line splits in two regions, see Figs.
5
(a) and
21
. At extremely high anisotropy,
t_{z}<0.002\,t
, pair formation is indeed dominated by logarithmic divergencies of 2D integrals. By that measure, the pairs may indeed be regarded as pure two-dimensional. However, when
t_{z}>0.002\,t
the divergencies are no longer dominant and the threshold is on the order of its value in an isotropic 3D lattice. For that reason, real-space pairs in this regime are already three-dimensional. Thus, two-body solutions may help to clarify the nature of superconductivity depending on the level of anisotropy observed in a system.

12.1.5
Stability of fermion pairs against phase separation

Phase separation is a ubiquitous feature of all models with finite-range attraction such as
UV
or
t-J
models. Physical reasoning suggests that any system with a strong enough attraction will form clusters and eventually phase-separate. It raises the question about stability of real-space pairs in many-body systems. A positive answer is provided by the Fermi statistics
[
35
,
144
,
148
,
149
,
147
]
. The ground state of a fermion pair is usually a spin singlet with a nodeless wave function. When two pairs attempt to coalesce into a quad, the full wave function must develop nodes. It is equivalent to a short-range
repulsion
between pairs. This additional repulsion keeps the pairs separate as long as attraction is not too strong. (Since this reasoning does not apply to bosons, Bose-
UV
models are
not
stable against phase separation
[
35
]
.)

The derived conclusion is very general and applicable to a wide class of inter-particle potentials including three-dimensional and long-range ones. It has been confirmed by exact numerical calculations for
t-J
models
[
33
,
34
]
,
UV
models
[
35
,
148
,
149
,
147
]
, and electron-phonon models
[
146
]
. Real-space pairs retain their identity in a
finite
interval of attraction above the binding threshold.

12.2
Relevance to cuprate superconductivity

Real-space pairs are known to adequately describe many aspects of high-
T_{c}
phenomenology. This topic is not discussed here and the reader is referred to available reviews
[
13
,
15
,
21
,
11
,
12
]
. We also note the recent observation of pairs in copper oxides
[
19
]
and iron-based superconductors
[
17
,
18
]
.

In this section, we discuss insights into cuprate superconductivity provided by two-body problems. The first one concerns the role of anisotropy. It was shown in Sec.
9.3
and Ref.
[
32
]
that the preformed pair mechanism naturally leads to the notion of
optimal anisotropy
. If
t_{z}
is large, then kinetic energy is large, attraction is below the threshold, and pairs do not form. Even if attraction is above the threshold, the binding is weak, pairs are large, close-packed density is small and
{\cal T}^{\ast}_{\rm BEC}
is small as a result. In the opposite limit of very small
t_{z}
, pairs are well formed and are compact but the out-of-plane mass is very large, which also reduces
{\cal T}^{\ast}_{\rm BEC}
. Thus, there
must
be an optimal
t_{z}
. Importantly, this argument does not exclude the existence of fully isotropic 3D superconductors like Ba
1-x
K
x
BiO
3
. Rather, it means that any isotropic superconductivity with low carrier density can be
improved upon
by reducing coupling between layers. That will result in more compact pairs and a higher
{\cal T}^{\ast}_{\rm BEC}
. We also note, in passing, that the level of anisotropy can be dramatically altered by electron-phonon interaction
[
150
]
or similar mediators.

The above argument applies equally to quasi-1D superconductivity where the crystal is composed of weakly bound 1D chains. In this respect, the recent claim of a
T_{c}=90
K superconductivity in a quasi-1D copper oxide is particularly noteworthy
[
151
]
.

Similar to anisotropy, there is an optimal strength of attraction. A weak attraction cannot form pairs but a strong attraction leads to clustering and phase separation. Aggregating available results on the tetragonal
UV
model
[
32
,
147
]
, one finds that a “working” interval of
|V|
is about
0.5\,t
. The interval is actually
U
-dependent, see Fig.
26
(b). It grows larger at small
U
but shrinks at very large
U>50\,t
. Then an optimal level of anisotropy
[
147
]
is
t_{z}<0.1\,t
. Let us assume
t=0.1
eV as a ballpark estimate of the in-plane hopping in physical units. (A corresponding total bandwidth is
\approx 1
eV.) Then the working interval of attraction is
\triangle V\approx 0.05
eV and the optimal interlayer hopping is
t_{z}<0.01
eV. Thus, the levels of attraction and anisotropy must both fall within narrow intervals to be close to optimal.
This may help explain the experimental fact that high-
T_{c}
superconductivity is a rare phenomenon only found in a few materials families.

Figure 28:
Evolution of a
UV
model with hole doping. The carrier hopping (right axis) increases from
t_{h}
near half-filling (
x=0
) to
t_{0}
in the empty lattice (
x=1
). Accordingly, the attraction strength
expressed in units of

t
(left axis) decreases from above threshold to below threshold.

Further insights can be gained by examining Fig.
24
. It clearly shows that the
peak

{\cal T}^{\ast}_{\rm BEC}
increases with
|V|
. It is beneficial for superconductivity to increase
|V|
all the way until phase separation. One concludes that
highest critical temperatures are expected in systems close to phase separation
. Since the system is close to phase separation globally, it may go over the threshold
locally
due to disorder, lattice defects, Jahn–Teller distortions
[
23
,
152
]
, and other factors. This may help explain proliferation of charge density waves, nematic orders, and other charge instabilities experimentally observed in the cuprates
[
153
,
154
,
155
,
156
,
157
]
.

Finally, we discuss the role played by strong electron correlations represented by Hubbard repulsion
U
. Consider the square lattice with bare electron hopping
t_{0}
. Near half-filling, the effective hopping of
holes
is reduced by strong correlations by a factor of
\sim 4-10

[
117
,
129
]
. As a consequence, a relatively weak attraction
|V|
that was unable to bind fast electrons moving with hopping
t_{0}
is now able to bind slow holes moving with a smaller effective hopping
t_{h}
.
The role of strong in-plane correlation is to further slow down the carriers to enable pair formation by a weak attraction.
In a recent analysis of thermodynamic measurements of several cuprate families, Harrison and Chan argued
[
130
]
that the in-plane hopping near half-filling is about
t_{h}=0.11
eV while it should be 0.36 eV according to electronic structure calculations. Such a band narrowing is due to electron correlations. Since the band structure value should be accurate in an empty lattice, one concludes that the effective
t
increases from 0.11 to 0.36 eV with doping. At the same time, quantum chemistry calculations
[
131
,
132
]
suggest an attractive potential of about 0.12 eV. Thus,
V/t\approx 1
near half-filling. This may be sufficient for binding in some highly anisotropic
UV
models.

Consider evolution of the system with hole doping starting at half-filling illustrated in Fig.
28
. By continuity, the effective carrier hopping must smoothly increase from
t_{h}
at
x=0
to
t_{0}
at
x=1
. Since exact shape of this curve is unknown, we show it schematically as a straight line. We then assume that the attraction strength
in physical units
stays approximately constant with doping. Then,
|V|
will be systematically
decreasing
when expressed in units of
t
. If
|V|
was above a pairing threshold at
x=0
, it will end up below the threshold at a finite
x
. At this doping, the pairs disappear. At some intermediate doping, the pairs balloon in size to reach close-packing. At this
optimal doping
, the critical temperature is maximal.
The preformed-pair mechanism naturally explains the existence of optimal doping and eventual disappearance of superconductivity with increasing hole density.
A recent analysis of the magnetic susceptibility and Knight shift in cuprates
[
158
]
suggests that the pseudogap decreases linearly with doping and coexists with superconductivity until both disappear in the overdoped regime. This conclusion supports our qualitative scenario. Returning to the specific numbers reported in Ref.
[
130
]
, let us assume the threshold to be
|V_{\rm cr}|=0.6\,t
. When
t
increases to
\approx 0.20
eV with doping, the pairs would evaporate. Thus, superconductivity should disappear by
x=0.36
.

12.3
Future outlook

Several additional two-body problems seem to be of interest. One of them is the body-centered tetragonal (BCT)
UV
model with out-of-plane attraction. The BCT lattice supports in-plane light pairs (but not out-of-plane ones), which should boost
T^{\ast}_{\rm BEC}
. The negative effects of
U
on pairing can be minimized by a small
t_{z}
. Compared with the simple tetragonal lattice, BCT has four times as many attractive bonds, which should reduce the threshold values by about a factor of four. Finally, BCT is close to the crystal structure of some cuprate superconductors.

It would also be interesting to analyze two-body problems with effective single-particle dispersions arising from strong correlations. For example, holes in nearly half-filled
t-J
models acquire the tendency to hop diagonally to second nearest neighbors
[
117
,
129
]
. According to Bak and Micnas
[
37
]
, such a dispersion may result in a
d
-symmetric ground state. Thus, such models may shed light on the symmetry of superconducting order parameter in cuprates.

Also of interest are multi-orbital models describing CuO
2
planes and even full CuO
6
octahedra. Only one such investigation has been published so far
[
104
]
. Extension of this work to 3D and accommodation of the available quantum-mechanical calculations of
t
,
U
, and
V

[
159
,
131
,
132
,
160
]
, would make the theory less phenomenological.

Finally, we mention the need to investigate a four-fermion problem in the tetragonal
UV
model to complete the picture of phase separation in 3D.

Declaration of competing interest

The author declares to not have known competing financial interests or personal relationships that could have appeared to influence the work reported in this paper.

Data availability

No data was used for the research described in the article.

Appendix A
Omissions and extensions

A.1
Miscellaneous two particle problems

In this section, we briefly describe other two-particle problems that have been left out of the present review.

Early results on bound pairs in 1D, 2D square, and 3D simple cubic
UV
models were derived by Micnas and reported in Sec. III.C of Ref.
[
11
]
.

Two-particle bound states in the square
t-J
model and
t-J-U
models, which map to the
UV
model, were studied by Lin
[
60
]
, Petukhov, Galán, and Vergés
[
61
]
, and Kagan and Rice
[
62
]
. All those works are consistent with the material of Section
5
.

Bak and Micnas
[
37
,
161
]
considered a square
UV
model with second nearest-neighbor
hopping
. They found that the latter could change the symmetry of the ground state. The
d
-symmetric pair had a lower energy than the
s
-symmetric one when the first-neighbor and second-neighbor hoppings were of opposite signs. Since effective second-neighbor hopping may be induced by strong correlations on an antiferromagnetic background near half-filling, this result may have implications for understanding symmetries of the superconducting order parameter in cuprates.

Marsiglio and Hirsch
[
124
]
developed and solved a two-body problem with
correlated hopping
, in which hopping of one particle depended on nearby presence of another particle.

In Ref.
[
162
]
, a square
UV
model with
anisotropic
next-nearest-neighbor attraction was studied in the context of cold atom quantum simulator of extended Hubbard–Holstein models.

A two-magnon problem with complicated second-neighbor hopping and interaction was solved in Ref.
[
86
]
.

A 3D
UV
model on the BCC lattice was considered in Ref.
[
89
]
. Pair mass, radius, and binding conditions were computed. Based on those results, a critical temperature of an atomic condensate in a BCC optical lattice was estimated to be around 10 nK.

In Ref.
[
90
]
, a
UV
model on the FCC lattice was analyzed in the context of superconducting fullerides. The pairs were found to be of small radius, strongly-bound, but relatively light due to the light-pair effect. It was estimated that such pairs could Bose-condense at high temperatures even if the lattice constant was large, as in the fullerides.

The following works studied
multi-orbital
models with on-site or intersite attractions.

A “mixed” repulsive-attractive one-dimensional Hubbard model was studied in Ref.
[
103
]
. The unit cell consisted of two non-equivalent sites. The first site hosted a Hubbard repulsion
U_{r}
whereas the second site hosted a Hubbard attraction
-|U_{a}|
. The singlet pairs were found to form when

|U_{a}|>\frac{2U_{r}t\sqrt{\cos{P}}}{U_{r}+2t\sqrt{\cos{P}}}\>.

(266)

Note an unusual, square-root, dependence on the pair momentum
P
. The latter is confined to the interval
-\frac{\pi}{2}\leq P\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
in this case.

In Ref.
[
39
]
, a
UV
model on a checker-board square lattice was studied. The symmetry between two sublattices was assumed to be broken by antiferromagnetic correlations of the underlying many-body system. The correlations created a staggered magnetic field
\Delta
that elevated energy of a spin-up hole on the first sublattice and lowered it on the second sublattice. For a spin-down hole, sublattice energies were reversed. It was found that a nonzero
\Delta
favored pairing because of two effects. First, the resulting band splitting reduced kinetic energies of the holes. Second, a nonzero
\Delta
suppressed double occupancy and reduced the influence of
U
. Both factors effectively increased the attraction
V
relative to
t
and
U
, which leads to easier pairing. A more detailed analysis of this two-particle lattice problem can be found in Ref.
[
100
]
.

Two-body bound states in multi-orbital models with
flat bands
were studied in Ref.
[
163
]
.

Finally, we mention the mixed Hubbard model on a multi-orbital
{\rm CuO}_{2}
plane
[
104
]
. The model included a repulsion on copper sites,
U_{\rm C}
, and an attraction on oxygen sites,
-|U_{\rm O}|
. The atomic energies of Cu and O sites were assumed equal,
\varepsilon_{\rm C}=\varepsilon_{\rm O}
, for both spin orientations. The pairing condition was found to be
|U_{\rm O}|>8\sqrt{2}U_{\rm C}t/(3U_{\rm C}+4\sqrt{2}t)
, where
t
is the absolute value of the hoping integral between the copper’s
d_{x^{2}-y^{2}}
orbital and oxygen’s
p_{x,y}
orbitals. One should add that multiple orbitals introduced significant technical complications in exact two-particle solutions, as explained in
A.2
. A similar analysis for the case of
oxygen–oxygen
attraction
[
164
]
resulted in a threshold condition,
|V_{OO}|>U_{C}t/(0.595U_{C}+2t)
. The latter model in its
many-body
version was recently studied by constrained-path Monte Carlo in Ref.
[
51
]
.

A.2
Extension to non-Bravais lattices and multi-orbital models

When two fermions interact within a complex lattice with
S>1
orbitals per unit cell, an exact solution becomes considerably more complicated. Systematic investigation of such models are beyond the scope of this work. In this section, we briefly outline the procedure and point out how it differs from the basic
S=1
case.

Assuming the index
{\bf m}
continues to number
unit cells
, the two-body wave function
\Psi_{\alpha\beta}({\bf m}_{1},{\bf m}_{2})
comprises
S^{2}
components arranged in an
(S^{2}\times 1)
array. The Schrödinger equation, Eqs. (
4
) and (
7
), comprises
S^{2}
coupled equations. The right-hand-side of Eq. (
7
) still contains a
finite
number of integrals
\Phi({\bf P})
which, similarly to Eq. (
12
), allows expressing
\psi_{{\bf k}_{1}\alpha,{\bf k}_{2}\beta}
as a linear combination of
\Phi({\bf P})
. However, the respective energy-dependent coefficients are now components of an inverted
(S^{2}\times S^{2})
matrix. Each coefficient is a ratio of an
(S^{2}-1)
-degree polynomial of
E
and an
S^{2}
-degree polynomial of
E
. The denominator can be factorized into a product of
S^{2}
factors
(E-\varepsilon_{\alpha{\bf k}_{1}}-\varepsilon_{\beta{\bf k}_{2}})
, where
\varepsilon_{\alpha{\bf k}}
are the
S
bands of the single-particle dispersion. When
\varepsilon_{\alpha{\bf k}}
is known analytically, factorization can also be performed analytically, at least in principle. Then each coefficient can be expanded into a sum of
S^{2}
simple fractions. Finally, substitution of
\psi_{{\bf k}_{1}\alpha,{\bf k}_{2}\beta}
into the definitions of
\Phi({\bf P})
produces a finite set of algebraic equations for
\Phi({\bf P})
but with matrix elements
M
being sums of
S^{2}
terms where each term has the general form of Eq. (
15
) with denominators
(-E+\varepsilon_{\alpha{\bf q}}+\varepsilon_{\beta,{\bf P}-{\bf q}})
and additional functions of
{\bf q}
in numerators.

In practice, this program can be completed for the simplest multiorbital models only
[
39
,
103
,
104
,
100
]
. Often enough,
\varepsilon_{\alpha{\bf k}}
cannot be calculated analytically, and the above procedure pauses after the matrix inversion but before factorization. From that stage, everything must be computed numerically.

Appendix B
Green’s functions of the 2D square and rectangular lattices

B.1
Definitions

In this section, we will be concerned with analytical evaluation of the two dimensional integrals

M^{\rm rt}_{nm}(E;\alpha,\beta)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\bf q}\frac{\cos{nq_{x}}\cos{mq_{y}}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{q_{y}}}=\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{\cos{nq_{x}}\cos{mq_{y}}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{q_{y}}}\>.

(267)

Here
\alpha\equiv 4t_{x}\cos{\frac{P_{x}}{2}}\geq 0
and
\beta\equiv 4t_{y}\cos{\frac{P_{y}}{2}}\geq 0
.
t_{x,y}>0
are absolute values of hopping integrals along
x
and
y
axes, respectively.
P_{x}
and
P_{y}
are two components of a total pair quasi-momentum. Since
-\pi\leq P_{x},P_{y}\leq+\pi
, both
\alpha
and
\beta
are non-negative. The quantities
M^{\rm rt}_{nm}(E;\alpha,\beta)
are
Green’s functions
of the rectangular lattice. However, we prefer not to use the term “Green’s function” in the main text of this study because our definition, Eq. (
267
), differs from the standard one by a constant negative factor. The superscript “rt” indicates that these
M_{nm}
belong to the rectangular lattice. When
\alpha=\beta
, a superscript “sq” will be used, indicating Green’s functions of the square lattice. The rectangular functions, Eq. (
267
), appear in the
square
two-body problem because the center-of-mass motion breaks the
x-y
symmetry of the underlying lattice. We will be interested only in the discrete energy spectrum with
E\leq-(\alpha+\beta)
. For those energies, all
M^{\rm rt}_{nm}
are real, positive, and logarithmically diverge as
E\rightarrow-(\alpha+\beta)
from below. Since
M^{\rm rt}_{-n,m}=M^{\rm rt}_{nm}
and
M^{\rm rt}_{n,-m}=M^{\rm rt}_{nm}
, only
M^{\rm rt}_{nm}
with non-negative
n
and
m
need to be evaluated.

All
M^{\rm rt}_{nm}
are eventually expressible via the three complete elliptic integrals
{\bf K}
,
{\bf E}
, and
{\bf\Pi}
. In this paper, the following definitions are used

{\bf K}(\kappa)=\int\limits^{\pi/2}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}\phi}{\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}\sin^{2}{\!\phi}}}\>;\hskip 8.5359pt{\bf E}(\kappa)=\int\limits^{\pi/2}_{0}\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}\sin^{2}{\!\phi}}\>{\rm d}\phi\>;\hskip 8.5359pt{\bf\Pi}(n,\kappa)=\int\limits^{\pi/2}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}\phi}{(1-n\sin^{2}{\!\phi})\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}\sin^{2}{\!\phi}}}\>.

(268)

Expressions of
M^{\rm rt}_{nm}
via the generalized hypergeometric function can be found in Ref.
[
165
]
. Rectangular Green’s functions with second-neighbor hopping were evaluated in Ref.
[
161
]
.

B.2
Basic integrals for the square case,
\alpha=\beta

We begin with calculating the basic integral
M^{\rm sq}_{00}
for the isotropic case,
\alpha=\beta
. First integration utilizes the residue theorem with the result

M^{\rm sq}_{00}(E;\alpha,\alpha)=\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{(|E|+\alpha-\alpha\cos{q_{x}})(|E|-\alpha-\alpha\cos{q_{x}})}}=\frac{1}{\pi\alpha}\int^{1}_{-1}\frac{{\rm d}z}{\sqrt{(1-z^{2})(z_{1}-z)(z_{2}-z)}}\>,

(269)

where
z_{1}=(|E|+\alpha)/\alpha
and
z_{2}=(|E|-\alpha)/\alpha
. Then two successive transformations of the integration variable

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=-1+\frac{1}{u}\>,

(270)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{z_{1}+1}+\frac{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{z_{1}+1}}{\sin^{2}{\phi}}\>,

(271)

result in

M^{\rm sq}_{00}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=\frac{2}{\pi|E|}\>{\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right).

(272)

To calculate
M^{\rm sq}_{10}
, we first compose the sum rule

M^{\rm sq}_{n+1,m}(E;\alpha,\alpha)+M^{\rm sq}_{n-1,m}(E;\alpha,\alpha)+M^{\rm sq}_{n,m-1}(E;\alpha,\alpha)+M^{\rm sq}_{n,m+1}(E;\alpha,\alpha)=-\frac{2}{\alpha}\>\delta_{n0}\delta_{m0}+\frac{2|E|}{\alpha}\>M^{\rm sq}_{nm}(E;\alpha,\alpha)\>,

(273)

which can be verified by direct substitution. For
n=m=0
and
\alpha=\beta
, all four terms in the left-hand-side are equal by symmetry, which leads to

M^{\rm sq}_{10}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=\frac{|E|}{2\alpha}\>M^{\rm sq}_{00}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}=\frac{1}{\pi\alpha}\>{\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\>.

(274)

Calculation of the next basic integral,
M^{\rm sq}_{20}
, is more involved. Since the following derivation will also be needed in the anisotropic case,
\alpha\neq\beta
, we present here its general version. After integration over
q_{y}
, which is elementary,

M^{\rm sq}_{20}(E;\alpha,\beta)=\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\frac{2\cos^{2}{\!q_{x}}-1}{\sqrt{(|E|+\beta-\alpha\cos{q_{x}})(|E|-\beta-\alpha\cos{q_{x}})}}\>.

(275)

The second term here is
M^{\rm sq}_{00}
. It will be moved, temporarily, to the left side of the equation. After the substitution
\cos{q_{x}}=z
,

M^{\rm sq}_{20}(E;\alpha,\beta)+M^{\rm sq}_{00}(E;\alpha,\beta)=\frac{2}{\pi\alpha}\int^{1}_{-1}\frac{z^{2}\>{\rm d}z}{\sqrt{(1-z^{2})(z_{1}-z)(z_{2}-z)}}\>,

(276)

where
z_{1}=(|E|+\beta)/\alpha
and
z_{2}=(|E|-\beta)/\alpha
. Next we use substitution
z=z_{1}+\frac{1}{u}
to convert the quartic polynomial under the square root into a cubic polynomial. The result is

M^{\rm sq}_{20}(E;\alpha,\beta)+M^{\rm sq}_{00}(E;\alpha,\beta)=\frac{2}{\pi\alpha}\sqrt{abc}\>\left\{\left(\frac{|E|+\beta}{\alpha}\right)^{2}\!\!J_{0}-2\frac{|E|+\beta}{\alpha}\>J_{-1}+J_{-2}\right\},

(277)

where

a\equiv\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}\>,\hskip 28.45274ptb\equiv\frac{\alpha}{|E|+\beta-\alpha}\>,\hskip 28.45274ptc\equiv\frac{\alpha}{|E|+\beta+\alpha}\>,\hskip 28.45274pta>b>c\>,

(278)

J_{m}=\int^{b}_{c}\frac{u^{m}\>{\rm d}u}{\sqrt{(a-u)(b-u)(u-c)}}\>.

(279)

According to the general theory of elliptic integrals
[
166
]
,
J_{-2}
can expressed as a linear combination of three fundamental integrals
J_{-1}
,
J_{0}
and
J_{1}
. To this end, call the polynomial under the square root
Q(u)
, calculate the derivative
\frac{d}{du}[\frac{1}{u}\sqrt{Q(u)}]
and integrate the result between
u=c
and
u=b
. This leads to

J_{-2}=\frac{1}{2abc}\>\left\{\left(ab+ac+bc\right)\!J_{-1}-J_{1}\right\}.

(280)

Substitution of Eq. (
280
) in Eq. (
277
) yields

M^{\rm sq}_{20}(E;\alpha,\beta)+M^{\rm sq}_{00}(E;\alpha,\beta)=\frac{2}{\pi\alpha}\sqrt{abc}\>\left\{\left(\frac{|E|+\beta}{\alpha}\right)^{2}\!\!J_{0}-\frac{|E|}{\alpha}\>J_{-1}-\frac{1}{2abc}\>J_{1}\right\}.

(281)

As the next step, we use the substitution

u=c+(b-c)\sin^{2}{\!\phi}\>,

(282)

to transform
J_{m}
’s into Legendre normal forms, Eq. (
268
). The results are

\displaystyle J_{0}(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{a-c}}\>{\bf K}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{b-c}{a-c}}\right),

(283)

\displaystyle J_{1}(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{a-c}}\left\{a\>{\bf K}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{b-c}{a-c}}\right)-(a-c)\>{\bf E}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{b-c}{a-c}}\right)\right\},

(284)

\displaystyle J_{-1}(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{c\sqrt{a-c}}\>{\bf\Pi}\!\left(-\frac{b-c}{c},\sqrt{\frac{b-c}{a-c}}\right).

(285)

Note that the first argument in
{\bf\Pi}
is negative. At this point, we return to the isotropic case,
\alpha=\beta
. Using
a
,
b
,
c
from Eq. (
278
), one obtains

\displaystyle J_{0}(\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\frac{|E|+2\alpha}{|E|}}\>{\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right),

(286)

\displaystyle J_{1}(\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\frac{|E|+2\alpha}{|E|}}\>\left\{{\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)-\frac{|E|}{|E|+2\alpha}\>{\bf E}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)\right\},

(287)

\displaystyle J_{-1}(\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\sqrt{2}\>(|E|+2\alpha)^{3/2}}{\alpha\sqrt{|E|}}\>{\bf\Pi}\!\left(-\frac{2\alpha}{|E|},\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right).

(288)

Notice that the expression for
J_{-1}(\alpha,\alpha)
involves
{\bf\Pi}
with their two arguments being equal by absolute value. In this special case,
{\bf\Pi}
can be reduced to
{\bf K}
. According to
[
167
]
, #17.7.22,

2{\bf\Pi}(-\kappa,\kappa)-{\bf K}(\kappa)=\frac{\pi}{2}\frac{1}{1+\kappa}\>,

(289)

which is valid for any
0\leq\kappa<1
. We can prove Eq. (
289
) by the following procedure. Go back to
M^{\rm sq}_{10}(E;\alpha,\beta)
and transform it in the same manner as
M^{\rm sq}_{20}
starting with Eq. (
275
). Instead of Eq. (
281
), we have

M^{\rm sq}_{10}(E;\alpha,\beta)=\frac{1}{\pi\alpha}\sqrt{abc}\>\left\{\frac{|E|+\beta}{\alpha}\>J_{0}-J_{-1}\right\}.

(290)

The isotropic version reads

M^{\rm sq}_{10}(E;\alpha,\alpha)=\frac{2}{\pi\alpha|E|}\>\left\{\left(|E|+\alpha\right){\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)-\left(|E|+2\alpha\right){\bf\Pi}\!\left(-\frac{2\alpha}{|E|},\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)\right\}.

(291)

Thus,
M^{\rm sq}_{10}
also contains a special case of
{\bf\Pi}(-\kappa,\kappa)
. However, we previously derived an expression for
M^{\rm sq}_{10}
that includes
{\bf K}(\kappa)
only. By comparing Eq. (
274
) and Eq. (
291
), one obtains

{\bf\Pi}\!\left(-\frac{2\alpha}{|E|},\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\>{\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)+\frac{\pi}{4}\frac{|E|}{|E|+2\alpha}\>,

(292)

which is Eq. (
289
) for
\kappa=\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}
. Finally, by substituting Eq. (
292
) in Eq. (
288
), then expressions for
J_{m}(\alpha,\alpha)
,
a
,
b
and
c
in Eq. (
281
), and utilizing the previously derived formula for
M^{\rm sq}_{00}(E,\alpha,\alpha)
, one arrives at the deceptively simple result:

M^{\rm sq}_{20}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=\frac{2}{\pi|E|}\>{\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)+\frac{|E|}{\alpha^{2}}\left\{\frac{2}{\pi}\>{\bf E}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)-1\right\}.

(293)

B.3
Recurrence relations for all
M_{nm}
in the square case,
\alpha=\beta

The sum rule, Eq. (
273
), and basic integrals
M^{\rm sq}_{00}
,
M^{\rm sq}_{10}
and
M^{\rm sq}_{20}
, derived in the previous section enable calculation of several more
M^{\rm sq}_{mn}
. Specifically, by setting
(n,m)=(1,0)
, one can obtain
M^{\rm sq}_{11}
. Then, by setting
(n,m)=(1,1)
, one can obtain
M^{\rm sq}_{21}=M^{\rm sq}_{12}
. Finally, by setting
(n,m)=(2,0)
, one can derive
M^{\rm sq}_{30}
. However, after that progress stops. Further extension of this method is impossible. Fortunately, there is a second, remarkable identity due to Morita
[
168
]
, that involves only
M_{n0}
’s and for our case reads

2n\!\left(2|E|^{2}-\alpha^{2}\right)\!M^{\rm sq}_{n0}-2\alpha|E|(2n+1)M^{\rm sq}_{n+1,0}-2\alpha|E|(2n-1)M^{\rm sq}_{n-1,0}+\alpha^{2}(n+1)M^{\rm sq}_{n+2,0}+\alpha^{2}(n-1)M^{\rm sq}_{n-2,0}=0\>.

(294)

This expression is valid for
n\neq 0
. Using expressions for
M^{\rm sq}_{00}
,
M^{\rm sq}_{10}
,
M^{\rm sq}_{20}
and
M^{\rm sq}_{30}
derived above, one can validate Eq. (
294
) for
n=1
. Then, by setting
n=2,3,\ldots
, one can calculate
M^{\rm sq}_{40}
,
M^{\rm sq}_{50}
, and so on, for any
n
. After that, application of the sum rule, Eq. (
273
), enables calculation of
any

M^{\rm sq}_{nm}
, at least in principle. Below, we list all
M^{\rm sq}_{nm}
for
n+m\leq 4
.

\displaystyle M^{\rm sq}_{00}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\pi|E|}\>{\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right),

(295)

\displaystyle M^{\rm sq}_{10}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=M^{\rm sq}_{01}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi\alpha}\>{\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\>,

(296)

\displaystyle M^{\rm sq}_{11}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=

\displaystyle\frac{|E|}{\pi\alpha^{2}}\left\{\left(1-\frac{2\alpha^{2}}{|E|^{2}}\right){\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)-{\bf E}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)\right\},

(297)

\displaystyle M^{\rm sq}_{20}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=M^{\rm sq}_{02}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\pi|E|}\>{\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)+\frac{|E|}{\alpha^{2}}\left\{\frac{2}{\pi}\>{\bf E}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)-1\right\},

(298)

\displaystyle M^{\rm sq}_{21}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=M^{\rm sq}_{12}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{|E|^{2}}{\pi\alpha^{3}}-\frac{3}{\pi\alpha}\right\}{\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)-\frac{|E|^{2}}{\pi\alpha^{3}}\>{\bf E}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)+\frac{1}{2\alpha}\>,

(299)

\displaystyle M^{\rm sq}_{30}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=M^{\rm sq}_{03}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{9}{\pi\alpha}-\frac{2|E|^{2}}{\pi\alpha^{3}}\right\}{\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)+\frac{6|E|^{2}}{\pi\alpha^{3}}\>{\bf E}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)-\frac{2|E|^{2}}{\alpha^{3}}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\>,

(300)

\displaystyle M^{\rm sq}_{40}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=M^{\rm sq}_{04}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{2}{\pi|E|}+\frac{80|E|}{3\pi\alpha^{2}}-\frac{20|E|^{3}}{3\pi\alpha^{4}}\right\}{\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)

\displaystyle+\left\{\frac{8|E|}{3\pi\alpha^{2}}+\frac{44|E|^{3}}{3\pi\alpha^{4}}\right\}{\bf E}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)-\frac{4|E|}{\alpha^{2}}-\frac{4|E|^{3}}{\alpha^{4}}\>,

(301)

\displaystyle M^{\rm sq}_{31}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=M^{\rm sq}_{13}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=

\displaystyle\left\{-\frac{2}{\pi|E|}-\frac{13|E|}{3\pi\alpha^{2}}+\frac{4|E|^{3}}{3\pi\alpha^{4}}\right\}{\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)

\displaystyle+\left\{-\frac{7|E|}{3\pi\alpha^{2}}-\frac{4|E|^{3}}{3\pi\alpha^{4}}\right\}{\bf E}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)+\frac{2|E|}{\alpha^{2}}\>,

(302)

\displaystyle M^{\rm sq}_{22}(E\leq-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)=

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{2}{\pi|E|}-\frac{8|E|}{3\pi\alpha^{2}}+\frac{2|E|^{3}}{3\pi\alpha^{4}}\right\}{\bf K}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right)

\displaystyle+\left\{\frac{4|E|}{3\pi\alpha^{2}}-\frac{2|E|^{3}}{3\pi\alpha^{4}}\right\}{\bf E}\!\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}\right).

(303)

Several comments are in order. (i)
M^{\rm sq}_{nm}(E;\alpha,\alpha)
describe not only bound
UV
pairs in the
\Gamma
point but on the Brillouin zone diagonal as well. This follows from the definitions of
\alpha
and
\beta
. (ii) All integrals have the analytical structure of
\alpha M^{\rm sq}_{nm}=Q_{1}(\kappa^{-1}){\bf K}(\kappa)+Q_{2}(\kappa^{-1}){\bf E}(\kappa)+Q_{3}(\kappa^{-1})
, where
Q_{1,2,3}
are polynomials and
\kappa=\frac{2\alpha}{|E|}
. The smallest power in all
Q_{i}
is
\geq-1
while the highest power grows with
n
and
m
. The overall complexity of the analytical expressions increases with
n
and
m
. All
M^{\rm sq}_{nm}
include a nonzero term
\propto{\bf K}(\kappa)
that logarithmically diverges when
E\rightarrow-2\alpha-0
, as expected from the definition, Eq. (
267
). However, coefficients by
{\bf K}(\kappa)
approach a universal limit
\frac{1}{\pi\alpha}
that is the same for
all

n
and
m
. As a result, all differences
M^{\rm sq}_{nm}-M^{\rm sq}_{n^{\prime}m^{\prime}}
are finite at
E=-2\alpha
. They are discussed in the next section.

B.4
M^{\rm sq}_{nm}-M^{\rm sq}_{00}
at threshold. Square case,
\alpha=\beta

As discussed in the main body of this paper, to determine a pairing threshold, energy
E
must be sent to the lowest energy of two free particles from below. For the isotropic square
UV
model, it means
E\rightarrow-2\alpha-0
. As shown in
B.4
, all
M^{\rm sq}_{nm}
logarithmically diverge in that limit and direct determination of the threshold is impossible. The procedure can be regularized by subtracting from all
M^{\rm sq}_{nm}
a common term that has the same logarithmic divergence. The subtrahend can be any one of
M^{\rm sq}_{nm}
or any other function that has the same logarithmic divergence. The most obvious choice is
M^{\rm sq}_{00}
. Accordingly, we introduce the following new functions

L^{\rm sq}_{nm}(\alpha,\alpha)\equiv M^{\rm sq}_{nm}(E=-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)-M^{\rm sq}_{00}(E=-2\alpha;\alpha,\alpha)\>,

(304)

that are finite for all
n
,
m
and
\alpha
. It can be deduced from the definitions, Eq. (
267
) and Eq. (
304
), that all
L^{\rm sq}_{nm}<0
.

There are two principle ways of calculating
L^{\rm sq}_{nm}
. For those
\{nm\}
for which full expression
M^{\rm sq}_{nm}
is known,
L^{\rm sq}_{nm}
is given by its non-singular part, utilizing
{\bf E}(1)=1
. But in general,
L^{\rm sq}_{nm}
can be calculated directly from the definition, Eq. (
267
), because in this special case the second integration is also elementary. Below, we list
L^{\rm sq}_{nm}
for
|n|+|m|\leq 5
.

\displaystyle L^{\rm sq}_{10}(\alpha,\alpha)=L^{\rm sq}_{01}(\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\alpha}=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\,0.5000000000\,\ldots\>,

(305)

\displaystyle L^{\rm sq}_{20}(\alpha,\alpha)=L^{\rm sq}_{02}(\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\,(\pi-2)}{\pi\alpha}=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\,0.7267604552\,\ldots\>,

(306)

\displaystyle L^{\rm sq}_{11}(\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\pi\alpha}=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\,0.6366197723\,\ldots\>,

(307)

\displaystyle L^{\rm sq}_{30}(\alpha,\alpha)=L^{\rm sq}_{03}(\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=-\frac{17\pi-48}{2\pi\alpha}=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\,0.8605627315\,\ldots\>,

(308)

\displaystyle L^{\rm sq}_{21}(\alpha,\alpha)=L^{\rm sq}_{12}(\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=-\frac{8-\pi}{2\pi\alpha}=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\,0.7732395447\,\ldots\>,

(309)

\displaystyle L^{\rm sq}_{40}(\alpha,\alpha)=L^{\rm sq}_{04}(\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=-\frac{120\pi-368}{3\pi\alpha}=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\,0.9539872947\,\ldots\>,

(310)

\displaystyle L^{\rm sq}_{31}(\alpha,\alpha)=L^{\rm sq}_{13}(\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=-\frac{46-12\pi}{3\pi\alpha}=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\,0.8807515881\,\ldots\>,

(311)

\displaystyle L^{\rm sq}_{22}(\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=-\frac{8}{3\pi\alpha}=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\,0.8488263631\,\ldots\>,

(312)

\displaystyle L^{\rm sq}_{50}(\alpha,\alpha)=L^{\rm sq}_{05}(\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1203\pi-3760}{6\pi\alpha}=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\,1.0258046581\,\ldots\>,

(313)

\displaystyle L^{\rm sq}_{41}(\alpha,\alpha)=L^{\rm sq}_{14}(\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=-\frac{160-49\pi}{2\pi\alpha}=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\,0.9647908947\,\ldots\>,

(314)

\displaystyle L^{\rm sq}_{32}(\alpha,\alpha)=L^{\rm sq}_{23}(\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=-\frac{8+3\pi}{6\pi\alpha}=-\frac{1}{\alpha}\,0.9244131815\,\ldots\>.

(315)

B.5
Basic integrals in the rectangular case,
\alpha\neq\beta

Evaluation of basic integrals in the general case,
\alpha\neq\beta
, parallels that of the isotropic case considered in
B.2
. First, integration over
q_{y}
, and two substitutions defined by Eqs. (
270
) and (
271
) yield

M^{\rm rt}_{00}(E;\alpha,\beta)=\frac{2}{\pi\sqrt{|E|^{2}-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}}\>{\bf K}(\kappa_{0})\>,

(316)

where

\kappa_{0}\equiv\sqrt{\frac{4\,\alpha\beta}{|E|^{2}-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}}\>.

(317)

In order to derive
M^{\rm rt}_{10}
and
M^{\rm rt}_{20}
, we apply the transformation sequence, Eqs. (
275
)-(
285
). The only additional information needed is a relation between
{\bf\Pi}(-n,\kappa)
and
{\bf\Pi}(+n,\kappa)
(
[
167
]
, # 17.7.17) which for our case reads

{\bf\Pi}\left(-\frac{2\alpha}{|E|+\beta-\alpha},\kappa\right)=\frac{2\beta}{|E|+\alpha+\beta}\>{\bf K}(\kappa)+\frac{|E|-\alpha-\beta}{|E|+\alpha+\beta}\>{\bf\Pi}\left(\frac{2\alpha}{|E|+\alpha-\beta},\kappa\right).

(318)

With that, the results are

\displaystyle M^{\rm rt}_{10}(E\leq-(\alpha+\beta);\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi\alpha\sqrt{|E|^{2}-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}}\left\{\left(|E|-\beta\right){\bf K}(\kappa_{0})-\left(|E|-\alpha-\beta\right){\bf\Pi}\left(n_{10},\kappa_{0}\right)\right\},

(319)

\displaystyle M^{\rm rt}_{20}(E\leq-(\alpha+\beta);\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi\alpha^{2}\sqrt{|E|^{2}-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}}\left\{\left(|E|-\beta\right)^{2}{\bf K}(\kappa_{0})+\left[|E|^{2}-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}\right]{\bf E}(\kappa_{0})\right.

\displaystyle\makebox[113.81102pt]{}\left.-2|E|\left(|E|-\alpha-\beta\right){\bf\Pi}\left(n_{10},\kappa_{0}\right)\right\},

(320)

\displaystyle n_{10}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\alpha}{|E|+\alpha-\beta}\>.

(321)

M^{\rm rt}_{01}
and
M^{\rm rt}_{02}
are obtained by permuting
\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta
:

\displaystyle M^{\rm rt}_{01}(E\leq-(\alpha+\beta);\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi\beta\sqrt{|E|^{2}-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}}\left\{\left(|E|-\alpha\right){\bf K}(\kappa_{0})-\left(|E|-\alpha-\beta\right){\bf\Pi}\left(n_{01},\kappa_{0}\right)\right\},

(322)

\displaystyle M^{\rm rt}_{02}(E\leq-(\alpha+\beta);\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi\beta^{2}\sqrt{|E|^{2}-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}}\left\{\left(|E|-\alpha\right)^{2}{\bf K}(\kappa_{0})+\left[|E|^{2}-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}\right]{\bf E}(\kappa_{0})\right.

\displaystyle\makebox[113.81102pt]{}\left.-2|E|\left(|E|-\alpha-\beta\right){\bf\Pi}\left(n_{01},\kappa_{0}\right)\right\},

(323)

\displaystyle n_{01}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\beta}{|E|+\beta-\alpha}\>.

(324)

Note that
n_{10}n_{01}=\kappa^{2}_{0}
. Next, we compose a sum rule that is an anisotropic version of Eq. (
273
):

\alpha\left\{M^{\rm rt}_{n+1,m}(\alpha,\beta)+M^{\rm rt}_{n-1,m}(\alpha,\beta)\right\}+\beta\left\{M^{\rm rt}_{n,m-1}(\alpha,\beta)+M^{\rm rt}_{n,m+1}(\alpha,\beta)\right\}=-2\,\delta_{n0}\delta_{m0}+2|E|\,M^{\rm rt}_{nm}(\alpha,\beta)\>.

(325)

It can be verified by direct substitution of the definitions, Eq. (
267
). The
n=m=0
version of the sum rule reads

|E|\,M^{\rm rt}_{00}(\alpha,\beta)-\alpha M^{\rm rt}_{10}(\alpha,\beta)-\beta M^{\rm rt}_{01}(\alpha,\beta)=1\>,

(326)

which is satisfied by the expressions given above. By setting
(n,m)=(1,0)
or
(0,1)
in Eq. (
325
), one can derive the first diagonal integral

M^{\rm rt}_{11}(E\leq-(\alpha+\beta);\alpha,\beta)=\frac{1}{\pi\alpha\beta\sqrt{|E|^{2}-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}}\left\{\left(|E|^{2}-\alpha^{2}-\beta^{2}\right){\bf K}(\kappa_{0})-\left[|E|^{2}-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}\right]{\bf E}(\kappa_{0})\right\}.

(327)

Equations (
316
), (
319
), (
320
), (
322
), (
323
) and (
327
) are sufficient to analyze the square
UV
model at arbitrary pair momenta away from the threshold. The same equations are instrumental in developing an efficient numerical method of computing similar integrals in 3D
UV
models, see
D.3
.

By using the identity
\frac{1}{c}=\int^{\infty}_{0}\exp{(-cz)}\,dz
, Eq. (
267
) can be transformed into an integral of a product of two Bessel functions:

M^{\rm rt}_{nm}(E;\alpha,\beta)=\int^{\infty}_{0}e^{-|E|z}\,I_{n}(\alpha z)I_{m}(\beta z)\,{\rm d}z\>.

(328)

Using this representation, expressions for
M^{\rm rt}_{00}(E;\alpha,\beta)
and
M^{\rm rt}_{11}(E;\alpha,\beta)
can also be found in integrals handbooks (see, e.g.,
[
169
]
, # 2.15.20.1), albeit without derivation.

B.6
Recurrence relations for the rectangular case,
\alpha\neq\beta

To proceed further, we need anisotropic analogs of the Morita recurrence relation
[
168
]
. Repeating the derivation twice, first time for
M^{\rm rt}_{n0}
and second time for
M^{\rm rt}_{0m}
, one obtains

\displaystyle 2n(2|E|^{2}+\alpha^{2}-2\beta^{2})M^{\rm rt}_{n0}-2\alpha|E|(2n+1)M^{\rm rt}_{n+1,0}-2\alpha|E|(2n-1)M^{\rm rt}_{n-1,0}

\displaystyle\makebox[113.81102pt]{}+\alpha^{2}(n+1)M^{\rm rt}_{n+2,0}+\alpha^{2}(n-1)M^{\rm rt}_{n-2,0}=0\>,\hskip 28.45274ptn\neq 0\>,

(329)

\displaystyle 2m(2|E|^{2}+\beta^{2}-2\alpha^{2})M^{\rm rt}_{0m}-2\beta|E|(2m+1)M^{\rm rt}_{0,m+1}-2\beta|E|(2m-1)M^{\rm rt}_{0,m-1}

\displaystyle\makebox[113.81102pt]{}+\beta^{2}(m+1)M^{\rm rt}_{0,m+2}+\beta^{2}(m-1)M^{\rm rt}_{0,m-2}=0\>,\hskip 28.45274ptm\neq 0\>.

(330)

Using Eqs. (
329
)-(
330
), one can derive all
M^{\rm rt}_{n0}
and
M^{\rm rt}_{0m}
, at least in principle. For example,

\displaystyle M^{\rm rt}_{30}(E\leq-(\alpha+\beta);\alpha,\beta)=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\pi\alpha^{3}\sqrt{|E|^{2}-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}}\left\{\left[(|E|-\beta)(|E|^{2}-3|E|\beta-\alpha^{2}+2\beta^{2})+|E|\alpha^{2}\right]{\bf K}(\kappa_{0})\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\>3\,|E|\left[|E|^{2}-(\alpha-\beta)^{2}\right]{\bf E}(\kappa_{0})-(4|E|^{2}-\alpha^{2}+2\beta^{2})(|E|-\alpha-\beta){\bf\Pi}(n_{10},\kappa_{0})\right\}.\makebox[14.22636pt]{}

(331)

M^{\rm rt}_{03}
is obtained from Eq. (
331
) by permuting
\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta
and
n_{10}\leftrightarrow n_{01}
.

Then, utilizing the sum rule, Eq. (
325
), one can derive all other
M^{\rm rt}_{nm}
. For example,

\displaystyle M^{\rm rt}_{21}(E\leq-(\alpha+\beta);\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{|E|}{\beta}\>M^{\rm rt}_{20}-\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}\left(M^{\rm rt}_{10}+M^{\rm rt}_{30}\right),

(332)

\displaystyle M^{\rm rt}_{12}(E\leq-(\alpha+\beta);\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{|E|}{\alpha}\>M^{\rm rt}_{02}-\frac{\beta}{2\alpha}\left(M^{\rm rt}_{01}+M^{\rm rt}_{03}\right),

(333)

and so on. These analytical expressions are useful in investigations of square, simple cubic, and tetragonal
UV
models with attraction beyond nearest neighbors.

B.7
Table of
M_{nm}-M_{00}
at threshold in the rectangular case,
\alpha\neq\beta

All
M^{\rm rt}_{nm}
derived in
B.5
and
B.6
diverge at pair binding threshold when
E=-\alpha-\beta
. To obtain meaningful results, a subtractive procedure must be used. We define

L^{\rm rt}_{nm}(\alpha,\beta)\equiv M^{\rm rt}_{nm}(E=-\alpha-\beta;\alpha,\beta)-M^{\rm rt}_{00}(E=-\alpha-\beta;\alpha,\beta)\>.

(334)

L^{\rm rt}_{nm}(\alpha,\beta)
can be derived either by taking the
E\rightarrow-\alpha-\beta
limit in the general expressions for
M^{\rm rt}_{nm}(\alpha,\beta)
, or by direct evaluation of the double integrals in definitions, Eq. (
334
). In the latter case, the second integration is elementary but can be laborious. Below, we list several
L^{\rm rt}_{nm}(\alpha,\beta)
for the lowest
(n,m)
:

\displaystyle L^{\rm rt}_{10}(\alpha,\beta)=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\pi\alpha}\arcsin{\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+\beta}}}\>,

(335)

\displaystyle L^{\rm rt}_{01}(\alpha,\beta)=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\pi\beta}\arcsin{\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{\alpha+\beta}}}\>,

(336)

\displaystyle L^{\rm rt}_{20}(\alpha,\beta)=

\displaystyle-\frac{4}{\pi\alpha}\left[\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\alpha}\arcsin{\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+\beta}}}-\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}}\right],

(337)

\displaystyle L^{\rm rt}_{02}(\alpha,\beta)=

\displaystyle-\frac{4}{\pi\beta}\left[\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\beta}\arcsin{\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{\alpha+\beta}}}-\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{\beta}}\right],

(338)

\displaystyle L^{\rm rt}_{11}(\alpha,\beta)=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\pi\sqrt{\alpha\beta}}\>,

(339)

\displaystyle L^{\rm rt}_{21}(\alpha,\beta)=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\pi\alpha}\left[\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\sqrt{\alpha\beta}}-\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\arcsin{\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+\beta}}}\right],

(340)

\displaystyle L^{\rm rt}_{12}(\alpha,\beta)=

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{\pi\beta}\left[\frac{\alpha+\beta}{\sqrt{\alpha\beta}}-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\arcsin{\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{\alpha+\beta}}}\right],

(341)

\displaystyle L^{\rm rt}_{22}(\alpha,\beta)=

\displaystyle-\frac{8}{3\pi\sqrt{\alpha\beta}}\>.

(342)

These expressions are needed in deriving the pairing threshold in the square
UV
model at nonzero pair momenta
[
87
]
.

Appendix C
UV
model on the 2D triangular lattice

C.1
Evaluation of basic integral
M^{\rm tr}_{00}
, Eq. (
15
), for the triangular lattice

The single-particle dispersion in the nearest-neighbor hopping approximation is

\varepsilon_{\bf k}=-2t\cos{k_{x}}-4t\cos{\left(\frac{k_{x}}{2}\right)\cos{\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}k_{y}}{2}\right)}}.

(343)

Substituting this into Eq. (
15
), shifting the integration variables
{\bf q}={\bf q}^{\prime}+\frac{\bf P}{2}
, and rescaling
q^{\prime}_{y}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\,q^{\prime\prime}_{y}
, the double integral
M^{\rm tr}_{00}
is transformed into an integral over the
-\pi\leq q_{x},q_{y}\leq\pi
square domain:

\displaystyle M^{\rm tr}_{00}(E,{\bf P})

\displaystyle=\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=

\displaystyle=\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{2\pi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{(|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}})^{2}-(\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}})^{2}-(\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}})^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\sqrt{\tilde{\alpha}\cos^{2}{q_{x}}+\tilde{\beta}\cos{q_{x}}+\tilde{\gamma}}}\>,

(344)

where

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=4t\cos{\frac{P_{x}}{2}}\>,

(345)

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=8t\cos{\frac{P_{x}}{4}}\cos{\frac{\sqrt{3}P_{y}}{4}}\>,

(346)

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=8t\sin{\frac{P_{x}}{4}}\sin{\frac{\sqrt{3}P_{y}}{4}}\>.

(347)

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}\>,

(348)

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}

\displaystyle=-2\alpha|E|-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}\>,

(349)

\displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}

\displaystyle=|E|^{2}-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}\>.

(350)

Integration over
q_{y}
in Eq. (
344
) was done by utilizing the residue theorem. The next step depends on whether the expression under the square root can be factorized into a product of two real factors. If
{\tilde{\beta}}^{2}>4\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\gamma}
, it is possible, and

\displaystyle\sqrt{\tilde{\alpha}\cos^{2}{q_{x}}+\tilde{\beta}\cos{q_{x}}+\tilde{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\alpha\sqrt{(z_{1}-\cos{\phi})(z_{2}-\cos{\phi})}\>,

(351)

\displaystyle z_{1,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\tilde{\alpha}}\left(-\tilde{\beta}\pm\sqrt{{\tilde{\beta}}^{2}-4\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\gamma}}\right).

(352)

Then the problem is reduced to Eq. (
269
), and the same transformations, Eqs. (
270
) and (
271
), result in

M^{\rm tr}_{00}=\frac{2}{\pi\alpha}\frac{1}{\sqrt{(z_{1}-1)(z_{2}+1)}}\>{\bf K}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{2(z_{1}-z_{2})}{(z_{1}-1)(z_{2}+1)}}\right).

(353)

In the ground state,
P_{x}=P_{y}=0
,
\alpha=4t
,
\beta=8t
,
\gamma=0
, and

z_{1,2}=\frac{1}{\alpha}\left\{(|E|+\alpha)\pm\sqrt{2\alpha|E|+3\alpha^{2}}\right\}.

(354)

Substitution in Eq. (
353
) yields

M^{\rm tr}_{00}=\frac{2}{\pi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|E_{0}|^{2}-48t^{2}+16t\sqrt{2t|E_{0}|+12t^{2}}}}\>{\bf K}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{32t\sqrt{2|E_{0}|t+12t^{2}}}{|E_{0}|^{2}-48t^{2}+16t\sqrt{2|E_{0}|t+12t^{2}}}}\right).

(355)

If
{\tilde{\beta}}^{2}<4\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\gamma}
, factorization is not possible. Reduction to Legendre standard form is achieved by consecutive application of Eq. (
270
) and

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}+\frac{p}{2}+q}\cdot\tan^{2}{\frac{\phi}{2}}\>,

(356)

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\beta}-2\tilde{\alpha}}{\tilde{\alpha}-\tilde{\beta}+\tilde{\gamma}}\>,

(357)

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\alpha}}{\tilde{\alpha}-\tilde{\beta}+\tilde{\gamma}}\>.

(358)

The final result is

M^{\rm tr}_{00}=\frac{2}{\pi}\frac{1}{\left[(\tilde{\alpha}+\tilde{\gamma})^{2}-{\tilde{\beta}}^{2}\right]^{1/4}}\>{\bf K}\!\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\left[1-\frac{\tilde{\gamma}-\tilde{\alpha}}{\sqrt{(\tilde{\alpha}+\tilde{\gamma})^{2}-{\tilde{\beta}}^{2}}}\right]}\right).

(359)

C.2
Numerical evaluation of
M^{\pm}_{\bf nm}
for the triangular lattice

In this section, the following integrals are utilized:

\displaystyle\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}x}{2\pi}\frac{1}{a-b\cos{x}-c\sin{x}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a^{2}-b^{2}-c^{2}}}\>,

(360)

\displaystyle\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}x}{2\pi}\frac{\cos{x}}{a-b\cos{x}-c\sin{x}}

\displaystyle=\frac{b}{b^{2}+c^{2}}\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{a^{2}-b^{2}-c^{2}}}-1\right),

(361)

\displaystyle\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}x}{2\pi}\frac{\sin{x}}{a-b\cos{x}-c\sin{x}}

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{b^{2}+c^{2}}\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{a^{2}-b^{2}-c^{2}}}-1\right),

(362)

\displaystyle\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}x}{2\pi}\frac{\cos{(2x)}}{a-b\cos{x}-c\sin{x}}

\displaystyle=\frac{b^{2}-c^{2}}{\sqrt{a^{2}-b^{2}-c^{2}}}\left(\frac{a-\sqrt{a^{2}-b^{2}-c^{2}}}{b^{2}+c^{2}}\right)^{2},

(363)

\displaystyle\int^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}x}{2\pi}\frac{\sin{(2x)}}{a-b\cos{x}-c\sin{x}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2bc}{\sqrt{a^{2}-b^{2}-c^{2}}}\left(\frac{a-\sqrt{a^{2}-b^{2}-c^{2}}}{b^{2}+c^{2}}\right)^{2},

(364)

\displaystyle\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}x}{\pi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{a\cos^{2}{x}+b\cos{x}+c}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\frac{1}{[(a+c)^{2}-b^{2}]^{1/4}}\>{\bf K}\!\left[\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{c-a}{\sqrt{(a+c)^{2}-b^{2}}}\right)}\right],

(365)

where
a,b,c>0
, and
a^{2}>b^{2}+c^{2}
is assumed. We also define

S\equiv\left(|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}\right)^{2}-\beta^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}-\gamma^{2}\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\>,

(366)

to simplify notation. The parameters
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
are defined in Eqs. (
345
)-(
347
).

The spin-singlet matrix elements are defined in Eqs. (
194
)-(
198
). The nearest-neighbor vectors are defined as:
{\bf 0}=(0,0)
;
{\bf b}_{+1}\equiv{\bf 1}=(1,0)
;
{\bf b}_{+2}\equiv{\bf 2}=\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)
;
{\bf b}_{+3}\equiv{\bf 3}=\left(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)
. Integration over
q_{y}
yields

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 00}=

\displaystyle\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\frac{1}{\sqrt{S}}\>,

(367)

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 01}=2M^{+}_{\bf 10}=

\displaystyle\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{2\cos{q_{x}}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=2\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\frac{\cos{q_{x}}}{\sqrt{S}}\>,

(368)

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 02}=2M^{+}_{\bf 20}=

\displaystyle\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{2\cos{\left(\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=

\displaystyle=2\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\frac{\beta\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}-\gamma\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}{\beta^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}+\gamma^{2}\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}\left(\frac{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}}{\sqrt{S}}-1\right),

(369)

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 03}=M^{+}_{\bf 30}=

\displaystyle\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{2\cos{\left(-\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=

\displaystyle=2\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\frac{\beta\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}+\gamma\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}{\beta^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}+\gamma^{2}\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}\left(\frac{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}}{\sqrt{S}}-1\right),

(370)

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 11}=

\displaystyle\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{2e^{iq_{x}}\cos{q_{x}}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=2\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\frac{\cos^{2}{q_{x}}}{\sqrt{S}}\>,

(371)

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 21}=

\displaystyle\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{2e^{i\left(\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}\cos{q_{x}}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=

\displaystyle=2\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\frac{\cos{q_{x}}(\beta\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}-\gamma\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}})}{\beta^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}+\gamma^{2}\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}\left(\frac{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}}{\sqrt{S}}-1\right),

(372)

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 12}=

\displaystyle\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{2e^{iq_{x}}\cos{\left(\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=M^{+}_{\bf 21}\>,

(373)

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 31}=

\displaystyle\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{2e^{i\left(-\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}\cos{q_{x}}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=

\displaystyle=2\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\frac{\cos{q_{x}}(\beta\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}+\gamma\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}})}{\beta^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}+\gamma^{2}\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}\left(\frac{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}}{\sqrt{S}}-1\right),

(374)

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 13}=

\displaystyle\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{2e^{iq_{x}}\cos{\left(-\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=M^{+}_{\bf 31}\>,

(375)

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 22}=

\displaystyle\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{2e^{i\left(\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}\cos{\left(\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=

\displaystyle=\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{S}}+\frac{(\beta+\gamma)^{2}\cos^{2}{q_{x}}+(\beta^{2}-\gamma^{2})\cos{q_{x}}-2\beta\gamma}{2\sqrt{S}}\left(\frac{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\sqrt{S}}{\beta^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}+\gamma^{2}\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}\right)^{2}\right\},

(376)

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 23}=

\displaystyle\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{2e^{i\left(\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}\cos{\left(-\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=

\displaystyle=\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\left\{\frac{\cos{q_{x}}}{\sqrt{S}}+\frac{(\beta^{2}-\gamma^{2})+(\beta^{2}+\gamma^{2})\cos{q_{x}}}{2\sqrt{S}}\left(\frac{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\sqrt{S}}{\beta^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}+\gamma^{2}\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}\right)^{2}\right\},

(377)

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 32}=

\displaystyle\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{2e^{i\left(-\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}\cos{\left(\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=M^{+}_{\bf 23}\>,

(378)

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 33}=

\displaystyle\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{2e^{i\left(-\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}\cos{\left(-\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=

\displaystyle=\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{S}}+\frac{(\beta-\gamma)^{2}\cos^{2}{q_{x}}+(\beta^{2}-\gamma^{2})\cos{q_{x}}+2\beta\gamma}{2\sqrt{S}}\left(\frac{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\sqrt{S}}{\beta^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}+\gamma^{2}\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}\right)^{2}\right\}.

(379)

These expressions can be used for efficient numerical evaluation of
M^{+}_{\bf nm}
.

The spin-triplet matrix elements
M^{-}_{\bf nm}
are defined in Eq. (
202
). The nearest-neighbor vectors are defined as:
{\bf b}_{-1}\equiv{\bf 1}=(1,0)
;
{\bf b}_{-2}\equiv{\bf 2}=\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)
;
{\bf b}_{-3}\equiv{\bf 3}=\left(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)
. Integration over
q_{y}
yields

\displaystyle M^{-}_{\bf 11}=

\displaystyle\;(-2\,i)\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{e^{iq_{x}}\sin{q_{x}}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=2\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\frac{\sin^{2}{q_{x}}}{\sqrt{S}}\>,

(380)

\displaystyle M^{-}_{\bf 12}=

\displaystyle\;(-2\,i)\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{e^{iq_{x}}\sin{\left(\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}

\displaystyle=\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\frac{(\beta+\gamma)\sin^{2}{q_{x}}}{\beta^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}+\gamma^{2}\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}\left(\frac{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}}{\sqrt{S}}-1\right),

(381)

\displaystyle M^{-}_{\bf 21}=

\displaystyle\;(-2\,i)\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{e^{i\left(\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}\sin{q_{x}}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=M^{-}_{\bf 12}\>,

(382)

\displaystyle M^{-}_{\bf 13}=

\displaystyle\;(-2\,i)\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{e^{iq_{x}}\sin{\left(-\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}

\displaystyle=\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\frac{(\gamma-\beta)\sin^{2}{q_{x}}}{\beta^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}+\gamma^{2}\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}\left(\frac{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}}{\sqrt{S}}-1\right),

(383)

\displaystyle M^{-}_{\bf 31}=

\displaystyle\;(-2\,i)\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{e^{i\left(-\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}\sin{q_{x}}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=M^{-}_{\bf 13}\>,

(384)

\displaystyle M^{-}_{\bf 22}=

\displaystyle\;(-2\,i)\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{e^{i\left(\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}\sin{\left(\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=

\displaystyle=\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{S}}-\frac{(\beta+\gamma)^{2}\cos^{2}{q_{x}}+(\beta^{2}-\gamma^{2})\cos{q_{x}}-2\beta\gamma}{2\sqrt{S}}\left(\frac{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\sqrt{S}}{\beta^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}+\gamma^{2}\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}\right)^{2}\right\},

(385)

\displaystyle M^{-}_{\bf 23}=

\displaystyle\;(-2\,i)\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{e^{i\left(\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}\sin{\left(-\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=

\displaystyle=\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\left\{\frac{\cos{q_{x}}}{\sqrt{S}}-\frac{(\beta^{2}-\gamma^{2})+(\beta^{2}+\gamma^{2})\cos{q_{x}}}{2\sqrt{S}}\left(\frac{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\sqrt{S}}{\beta^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}+\gamma^{2}\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}\right)^{2}\right\},

(386)

\displaystyle M^{-}_{\bf 32}=

\displaystyle\;(-2\,i)\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{e^{i\left(-\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}\sin{\left(\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=M^{-}_{\bf 23}\>,

(387)

\displaystyle M^{-}_{\bf 33}=

\displaystyle\;(-2\,i)\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\!\!\int\limits^{\pi}_{-\pi}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}\,{\rm d}q_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{e^{i\left(-\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}\sin{\left(-\frac{q_{x}}{2}+q_{y}\right)}}{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\beta\cos{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\cos{q_{y}}-\gamma\sin{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}\sin{q_{y}}}=

\displaystyle=\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{S}}-\frac{(\beta-\gamma)^{2}\cos^{2}{q_{x}}+(\beta^{2}-\gamma^{2})\cos{q_{x}}+2\beta\gamma}{2\sqrt{S}}\left(\frac{|E|-\alpha\cos{q_{x}}-\sqrt{S}}{\beta^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}+\gamma^{2}\sin^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}\right)^{2}\right\}.

(388)

C.3
Table of
M^{\rm tr}_{\bf nm}-M^{\rm tr}_{\bf 00}
at threshold at the
\Gamma
-point

We set
P_{x}=P_{y}=0
and
E=-12\,t
. Then
\alpha=4t
,
\beta=8t
and
\gamma=0
.
L^{+}_{\bf nm}
are defined in Eqs. (
206
)-(
208
). Utilizing Eqs. (
366
)-(
374
), elementary integration yields:

\displaystyle L^{+}_{\bf 10}=

\displaystyle\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\frac{\cos{q_{x}}-1}{\sqrt{(12t-4t\cos{q_{x}})^{2}-64t^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}}=-\frac{1}{12\,t}\>,

(389)

\displaystyle L^{+}_{\bf 11}=

\displaystyle\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\frac{\cos^{2}{q_{x}}-1}{\sqrt{(12t-4t\cos{q_{x}})^{2}-64t^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}}=\frac{3\sqrt{3}-2\pi}{3\pi t}\>,

(390)

\displaystyle L^{+}_{\bf 12}=

\displaystyle\int^{\pi}_{0}\frac{{\rm d}q_{x}}{\pi}\frac{\cos{q_{x}}(3-\cos{q_{x}})-2}{\sqrt{(12t-4t\cos{q_{x}})^{2}-64t^{2}\cos^{2}{\frac{q_{x}}{2}}}}=\frac{\pi-6\sqrt{3}}{12\pi t}\>.

(391)

C.4
Application of group theory

The dispersion relations, Eqs. (
193
) and (
201
), are quite complex and in general can be solved only numerically. However, at high symmetry points of the Brillouin zone there is enough simplification that some results can be derived analytically. The analysis is greatly aided by the theory of point groups. This section is devoted to the exposition of this subject. The triangular lattice is used as an example;
UV
models on other lattices can be analyzed similarly.

At the
\Gamma
-point (
{\bf P}=0
), the following relations among singlet matrix elements hold:

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 10}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=M^{+}_{\bf 20}(\Gamma)=M^{+}_{\bf 30}(\Gamma)\>,

(392)

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 01}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=M^{+}_{\bf 02}(\Gamma)=M^{+}_{\bf 03}(\Gamma)=2\,M^{+}_{\bf 10}(\Gamma)\>,

(393)

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 11}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=M^{+}_{\bf 22}(\Gamma)=M^{+}_{\bf 33}(\Gamma)\>,

(394)

\displaystyle M^{+}_{\bf 12}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=M^{+}_{\bf 21}(\Gamma)=M^{+}_{\bf 13}(\Gamma)=M^{+}_{\bf 31}(\Gamma)=M^{+}_{\bf 23}(\Gamma)=M^{+}_{\bf 32}(\Gamma)\>.

(395)

Thus, there remains only four independent integrals
M^{+}_{\bf 00}(\Gamma)
,
M^{+}_{\bf 10}(\Gamma)
,
M^{+}_{\bf 11}(\Gamma)
, and
M^{+}_{\bf 12}(\Gamma)
. Similarly, the following relations hold between triplet matrix elements:

\displaystyle M^{-}_{\bf 11}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=M^{-}_{\bf 22}(\Gamma)=M^{-}_{\bf 33}(\Gamma)\>,

(396)

\displaystyle M^{-}_{\bf 12}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=M^{-}_{\bf 21}(\Gamma)=M^{-}_{\bf 23}(\Gamma)=M^{-}_{\bf 32}(\Gamma)\>,

(397)

\displaystyle M^{-}_{\bf 13}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=M^{-}_{\bf 31}(\Gamma)=-M^{-}_{\bf 12}(\Gamma)\>,

(398)

leaving only two independent integrals
M^{-}_{\bf 11}(\Gamma)
and
M^{-}_{\bf 12}(\Gamma)
. As a result, the general Eqs. (
193
) and (
201
) acquire additional symmetries. It is clear that a bound pair at
{\bf P}=0
should possess a hexagonal symmetry, which implies the solutions can be classified according to the irreducible representations (irreps) of the point group
C_{6v}
. We now execute a textbook algorithm
[
170
]
of constructing linear combinations of functions
\Phi
that form bases of the irreps of
C_{6v}
.

Figure 29:
Symmetry operations of a bound pair in the triangular
UV
model (point group
C_{6v}
). Rotations are defined in the anti-clockwise direction.
\sigma
denote mirror reflections with respect to indicated lines. The dot-dashed oval illustrates the symmetric basis function
\Phi^{+}_{1}
.
